<details id="proof-stmt-energy-cone-mean"><summary>Proof of stmt-energy-cone-mean</summary>

*Proof.* Subtract strong convexity of $d(h,\cdot)^2$ from Equation <a href="#energy:radial-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:radial-identity">[energy:radial-identity]</a> in the identity $2\langle h,u\rangle=\lVert h\rVert^2+\lVert u\rVert^2-d(h,u)^2$. The quadratic terms cancel, proving concavity. Induction gives Equation <a href="#energy:mean-concavity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:mean-concavity">[energy:mean-concavity]</a>.

For the independent samples, condition on $m_{j-1}=u$. The barycenter variance inequality gives $\mathbb E[d(u,V_j)^2\mid u]\ge\lVert u\rVert^2+1$. Strong convexity of squared distance from the cone vertex therefore gives, with $a_j=\mathbb E\lVert m_j\rVert^2$, $$a_j\le(1-1/j)^2a_{j-1}+1/j^2.$$ Starting with $a_1=1$, induction yields $a_j\le1/j$. ◻

</details>

<a id="energy:uniform-bound"></a>
Consider now a sequence of table instances indexed by $\nu$ for which the conclusions of Proposition <a href="#prop:sampling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:sampling">20</a> hold with errors tending to zero, for every fixed test and every fixed positive integer $t$. Suppose their groups have geometric actions as above, and choose the normalized minimizing basepoints $x_\nu$. Write $\Omega_\nu=\Omega_{n_\nu}$ for the nonempty finite set of master matches and $\mu_\nu=\mu_{N_\nu}$ for its uniform distribution. Lemma <a href="#energy:displacement-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="energy:displacement-bound">23</a> gives an absolute constant $M$ such that $$\label{energy:uniform-bound}
 \lVert v_s\rVert\le M
 \quad\text{for all letters in all sufficiently large instances.}$$ All statements about the limit are unchanged by discarding finitely many instances. The actions, their spaces, and the functions in the next lemma may depend on the complete table instances.

<a id="stmt-lem-conditional-test"></a>
<div id="lem:conditional-test" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (Uniform conditional test). *Let $I$ and $\{e\}$ be disjoint sets of fields with admissible union. For each $\nu$, let $h_I$ be a function of the labels on $I$ with values in $T_{x_\nu}X_\nu$, and suppose $\lVert h_I\rVert\le B$ for a constant independent of $\nu$ and the match. For either fixed choice $\sigma(s)=s$ or $\sigma(s)=s^{-1}$, <a id="energy:conditional-test"></a>$$\label{energy:conditional-test}
 \limsup_{\nu\to\infty}
 \mathbb E_{\mu_\nu}
 \langle h_I,v_{\sigma(e)}\rangle\le0.$$ Here $e$ in a subscript denotes the letter occupying that field.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-conditional-test"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-conditional-test</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $t$. Use the coupling in Proposition <a href="#prop:sampling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:sampling">20</a>: it gives $t$ matches agreeing on $I$, each branch marginal at total variation distance at most $\eta_{\nu,t}$ from $\mu_\nu$, and their $e$-labels at distance at most $\eta_{\nu,t}$ from independent uniform letters, where $\eta_{\nu,t}\to0$. Increasing the error if necessary allows one bound for all these comparisons. Applying the fixed inversion $\sigma$ preserves this assertion.

The vector $h_I$ has the same value in every branch. Form the inductive mean $m_t$ of the branch vectors $v_{\sigma(e_j)}$. Equation <a href="#energy:mean-concavity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:mean-concavity">[energy:mean-concavity]</a> gives, for every coupled tuple, $$\frac1t\sum_{j=1}^t\langle h_I,v_{\sigma(e_j)}\rangle
 \le\langle h_I,m_t\rangle\le B\lVert m_t\rVert.$$ The last step uses only the norm bound; it does not assume that $h_I$ is independent of the leaves.

Under independent uniform leaves, Equation <a href="#energy:inductive-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:inductive-mean">[energy:inductive-mean]</a> applies by Equations <a href="#energy:variance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:variance">[energy:variance]</a> and <a href="#energy:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:normalization">[energy:normalization]</a>. Under the coupling, $\lVert m_t\rVert\le M$, by convexity of the ball of radius $M$. Consequently the total variation comparison changes the expected squared norm by at most $2M^2\eta_{\nu,t}$. The branch comparisons change the expectation of each scalar-product test by at most $2BM\eta_{\nu,t}$. It follows that $$\mathbb E_{\mu_\nu}\langle h_I,v_{\sigma(e)}\rangle
 \le B\sqrt{1/t+2M^2\eta_{\nu,t}}+2BM\eta_{\nu,t}.$$ First take the upper limit in $\nu$ with $t$ fixed, and then let $t\to\infty$. All error bounds used only $B$ and $M$, so the conclusion is uniform over the action-dependent functions in the statement. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Products of orbit configurations

Enlarge $\Gamma$ by adding, for each of its prescribed two-edge paths, the complementary two-edge path of its decoration square. Denote this fixed finite graph by $\widehat\Gamma$; its additional midpoint vertices are distinct abstract vertices. A master match gives a consistent orbit realization of this graph in $X_\nu$: choose one anchor at $x_\nu$, and send a vertex reached by the successive letters $s_1,\ldots,s_k$ to $s_1\cdots s_kx_\nu$. All core closed walks are relations, and the decoration square relations guarantee consistency on the added paths. Fixing one path for each vertex shows that these orbit points are at distance at most $LM$ from $x_\nu$, where $L$ depends only on $\widehat\Gamma$.

Take the finite product over the matches with metric <a id="energy:product-metric"></a>$$\label{energy:product-metric}
 d_\nu((y_m),(z_m))^2
 =\frac1{|\Omega_\nu|}\sum_{m\in\Omega_\nu}d(y_m,z_m)^2,$$ based at the tuple of minimizing basepoints. It is a complete $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ space. The vertex tuples have bounded distance from this basepoint. Fix a nonprincipal ultrafilter on the sequence index set $\mathbb N$ and take the pointed metric ultralimit $Z$. This space is complete by (Bridson and Haefliger 1999, Lemma I.5.53) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[BH1999](#ref-BH1999)], and it is $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ by (Bridson and Haefliger 1999, Corollary II.3.10(2)) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[BH1999](#ref-BH1999)]. The bounded vertex tuples give a configuration in $Z$ indexed by the vertices of $\widehat\Gamma$. Midpoints and fixed-fraction points on segments in the products are formed coordinatewise, and their ultralimits are the corresponding points in $Z$ by uniqueness of geodesics. We identify the abstract vertex names with the resulting points. Neither properness nor a dimension bound for $Z$ is needed.

<a id="stmt-energy-path-bounds"></a>
<div id="energy:path-bounds" class="lemma">

**Lemma 26**. *Every edge in $\widehat\Gamma$ has length one in $Z$. Every two-step path using two distinct fields of one decoration square has squared endpoint distance at least two. More generally, a path of $k\le3$ distinct core fields whose field set is admissible has squared endpoint distance at least $k$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-energy-path-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-energy-path-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* For an edge with letter $s$, translation at its initial orbit vertex identifies its squared length with $\lVert v_s\rVert^2$, or with the same quantity for $s^{-1}$. Single-field total variation convergence, Equation <a href="#energy:uniform-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:uniform-bound">[energy:uniform-bound]</a>, and the normalization show that its squared product length tends to one.

For a path with oriented readings $a_1,\ldots,a_k$, translate its penultimate orbit vertex to $x_\nu$. Its earlier endpoint then becomes $a_{k-1}^{-1}\cdots a_1^{-1}x_\nu$, and its next endpoint becomes $a_kx_\nu$. Set $$h_I=\log_{x_\nu}(a_{k-1}^{-1}\cdots a_1^{-1}x_\nu).$$ This is a function of precisely the preceding labels, and $\lVert h_I\rVert\le(k-1)M$. Equation <a href="#energy:log-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:log-comparison">[energy:log-comparison]</a> gives $$d(u_0,u_k)^2\ge d(u_0,u_{k-1})^2+\lVert v_{a_k}\rVert^2
       -2\langle h_I,v_{a_k}\rangle$$ in every factor. Average, apply Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-test">25</a>, and take the ultralimit. Induction adds at least one at each step. Every prefix of the field sets in the statement is admissible, so the conditional test is available at each step. Distinct fields here need not carry distinct numerical letter values. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Quadrilateral equality and hexagon centers

The path estimates give lower bounds on squared distances. We now compare them with a $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ upper bound on the sum of the two diagonal squares of a quadrilateral. Equality will first determine the diagonals of every decoration square, and then force the three opposite pairs in each core hexagon to share a midpoint.

<a id="stmt-energy-quadrilateral"></a>
<div id="energy:quadrilateral" class="lemma">

<a id="energy:quadrilateral-inequality"></a>
**Lemma 27**. *For four points $z_1,z_2,z_3,z_4$ in a $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ space, let $m=\operatorname{mid}(z_1,z_3)$ and $n=\operatorname{mid}(z_2,z_4)$. Then $$\label{energy:quadrilateral-inequality}
 d(z_1,z_3)^2+d(z_2,z_4)^2+4d(m,n)^2
 \le\sum_{i=1}^4d(z_i,z_{i+1})^2,
 \qquad z_5=z_1.$$ In particular, equality between the two diagonal-square sum and the side-square sum forces the diagonal midpoints to coincide.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-energy-quadrilateral"><summary>Proof of stmt-energy-quadrilateral</summary>

*Proof.* Apply midpoint convexity to $d(m,z_2)^2$ and $d(m,z_4)^2$ using the segment $z_1z_3$, and then to $d(m,n)^2$ using $z_2z_4$. Substituting the first two inequalities into the third and multiplying by four gives Equation <a href="#energy:quadrilateral-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:quadrilateral-inequality">[energy:quadrilateral-inequality]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="energy:two-step-equality"></a>
Each decoration square has unit sides. Both of its diagonals have squared length at least two by Lemma <a href="#energy:path-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="energy:path-bounds">26</a>. Lemma <a href="#energy:quadrilateral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="energy:quadrilateral">27</a> forces both squared lengths to equal two. In particular, $$\label{energy:two-step-equality}
 d(u,w)^2=2
 \quad\text{for every nonbacktracking two-edge core path }u,v,w.$$ Every such path was assigned a decoration table, including paths whose two fields lie in the same fan.

Recall that a core hexagon is obtained from three noncollinear Fano points $i,j,k$, their joining lines, and a choice of $X$ or an available $Y$ representative at each of the three points, with all six required edges present. The squared distances of its vertices $z_0,\ldots,z_5$ at cyclic separation one and two are, respectively, one and two. Each three-edge half-path uses at most two fields of any one fan, so its opposite endpoints have squared distance at least three.

Apply Lemma <a href="#energy:quadrilateral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="energy:quadrilateral">27</a> to $(z_0,z_1,z_3,z_4)$. The side-square sum is $1+2+1+2=6$, whereas each diagonal-square is at least three. Both are therefore three and their midpoints coincide. Applying the same argument to $(z_1,z_2,z_4,z_5)$ identifies the third opposite-pair midpoint with the first two. Denote the resulting center of this hexagon by $c$. Every vertex has distance <a id="energy:radius"></a>$$\label{energy:radius}
 r=\frac{\sqrt3}{2}$$ from $c$.

<a id="stmt-energy-hexagon-flatness"></a>
<div id="energy:hexagon-flatness" class="lemma">

<a id="energy:hexagon-products"></a>
**Lemma 28**. *For a core hexagon, every triangle formed by its center and two of its vertices is Euclidean flat, including the degenerate case of opposite vertices. The corresponding tangent vectors satisfy $$\label{energy:hexagon-products}
 \langle \log_c z_i,\log_c z_j\rangle
 =r^2-\frac12d(z_i,z_j)^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-energy-hexagon-flatness"><summary>Proof of stmt-energy-hexagon-flatness</summary>

<a id="energy:antipodal-products"></a>
*Proof.* The opposite pairs are geodesic segments through $c$, so their directions in $T_cZ$ are antipodal and their radial lengths are $r$. If $v^+$ and $v^-$ are any such opposite vectors, the angular triangle inequality implies $$\label{energy:antipodal-products}
 \langle u,v^+\rangle+\langle u,v^-\rangle\le0
 \quad(u\in T_cZ).$$ Indeed, the two angles from $u$ to their directions have sum at least $\pi$, and the sum of their cosines is nonpositive.

Set $u=\log_c z_i$, $v^+=\log_c z_j$, and $v^-=\log_c z_{j+3}$, with indices modulo six. Log comparison gives $$\langle u,v^+\rangle\ge r^2-\tfrac12d(z_i,z_j)^2,
 \qquad
 \langle u,v^-\rangle\ge r^2-\tfrac12d(z_i,z_{j+3})^2.$$ The two prescribed squared distances sum to $3=4r^2$, so the lower bounds sum to zero. Equation <a href="#energy:antipodal-products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:antipodal-products">[energy:antipodal-products]</a> forces equality in each bound. Thus the angle at $c$ equals its Euclidean comparison angle. For a nondegenerate triangle the flat-triangle equality theorem gives a flat triangle; for an opposite or repeated pair the statement is a segment or a point. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-energy-common-center"></a>
<div id="energy:common-center" class="lemma">

**Lemma 29**. *All core hexagons have the same center $c$. Every core vertex lies in a core hexagon, and hence is at distance $r$ from $c$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-energy-common-center"><summary>Proof of stmt-energy-common-center</summary>

*Proof.* First use only $X$ representatives. A noncollinear ordered triple of Fano points is a basis of $\mathbb F_2^3$. Reordering a basis does not change its hexagon, and replacing one basis vector $i$ by $i+j$ preserves the line $ij$ and the third point $k$. The old and new hexagons therefore share the opposite pair $(X_k,ij)$, and their centers are its common midpoint. Permutations and elementary additions generate all changes of basis over $\mathbb F_2$, so the centers of all $X$ hexagons agree.

Replace one $Y$ representative in a core hexagon by its $X$ representative. The new hexagon is a core hexagon, since every corresponding $X$ edge exists. Either of the unchanged point representatives, together with its opposite line, is an opposite pair shared by the two hexagons. Their centers agree. Repeating this replacement connects every core hexagon to one using only $X$ representatives.

Every $X_i$ lies in a hexagon obtained by extending $i$ to a basis. Every line lies in one by choosing two points spanning that line and a third point outside it. Each $Y_i$ has two distinct incident lines available. Choose a point other than $i$ on each of them; these two points together with $i$ are noncollinear and yield a core hexagon containing $Y_i$. This includes $Y_D$, using its two full lines. The radius assertion follows. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### The ternary midpoint test

The square and hexagon comparisons have supplied a common center. We now use three admissible core fields to control one further angle: the angle at $CD$ between the segment to the midpoint of $X_C,Y_C$ and the loose edge to $X_D$.

Put $$H_0=CD,\qquad p=X_C,\qquad q=Y_C,\qquad z=X_D,
 \qquad J=\operatorname{mid}(p,q)$$ in $Z$. The following argument gives an angle statement in $Z$ without any assumption that angles commute with ultralimits.

Choose another line $H\ne H_0$ through $C$. The core four-cycle $(p,H_0,q,H)$ has unit sides and both diagonals of squared length two, by Equation <a href="#energy:two-step-equality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:two-step-equality">[energy:two-step-equality]</a>. Its diagonal midpoints coincide by Lemma <a href="#energy:quadrilateral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="energy:quadrilateral">27</a>, so <a id="energy:ternary-radii"></a>$$\label{energy:ternary-radii}
 J=\operatorname{mid}(H_0,H),\qquad
 d(H_0,J)^2=\frac12,\qquad d(H_0,z)=1.$$

In each orbit factor let $J_m$ be the midpoint of the realizations of $p$ and $q$. Translate the realization of $H_0$ to $x_\nu$. The realizations of $p,q$ are then given by the two letters on the adjacent $F_C$ edges, with their fixed orientation signs. Therefore the translated vector toward $J_m$ is a function $h_I$ of just those two fields, with norm at most $M$. The translated vector toward $z$ is $v_{\sigma(e_0)}$, where $e_0$ is the loose field. These three fields are admissible. Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-test">25</a> shows that the upper limit of the averaged scalar product of these two vectors is at most zero.

<a id="energy:fractional-angle"></a>
Fix $a,b\in(0,1]$. In each factor take the points at fractions $a,b$ along the segments from $H_0$ to $J_m,z$. Their tangent vectors are the corresponding scalar multiples of the two vectors just considered. Apply Equation <a href="#energy:log-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:log-comparison">[energy:log-comparison]</a>, average over factors, and take the ultralimit. Denoting the limiting fractional points by $J_a,z_b$, respectively, gives $$\label{energy:fractional-angle}
 d(J_a,z_b)^2\ge
 a^2d(H_0,J)^2+b^2d(H_0,z)^2
 =\frac{a^2}{2}+b^2.$$ Thus every such comparison angle at $H_0$ is at least $\pi/2$. Letting both fractions decrease to zero in $Z$ proves <a id="energy:ternary-angle"></a>$$\label{energy:ternary-angle}
 \angle_{H_0}(J,z)\ge\frac\pi2.$$ Only distances of fixed-fraction points passed through the ultralimit; the angle was computed afterwards in the limiting space.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### The resulting finite configuration

We collect the conclusions in the form needed in Section <a href="#sec:obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:obstruction">6</a>.

<a id="stmt-prop-configuration"></a>
<div id="prop:configuration" class="proposition">

**Proposition 30**. *Let $P_\nu$ be a sequence of aspherical instances whose master-match statistics and six-letter relative counts satisfy the conclusions of Proposition <a href="#prop:sampling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:sampling">20</a>, with errors tending to zero for every fixed test and every fixed $t$. If every $\pi_1(P_\nu)$ has a proper cocompact isometric action on a proper complete $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ space, then there are a complete $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ space $Z$, a configuration indexed by the core vertices of $\Gamma$, and a point $c\in Z$ with the following properties.*

1.  *Every core edge has length one. Every nonbacktracking two-edge core path has squared endpoint distance two. Every three-edge core path with distinct admissible fields has squared endpoint distance at least three.*

2.  *Every core hexagon has its three opposite-pair midpoints equal to $c$. All core vertices have distance $r=\sqrt3/2$ from $c$. For any two vertices occurring in one core hexagon, their central triangle with $c$ is flat and their tangent scalar product is given by Equation <a href="#energy:hexagon-products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:hexagon-products">[energy:hexagon-products]</a>.*

3.  *With $H_0=CD$ and $J=\operatorname{mid}(X_C,Y_C)$, one has $$d(H_0,J)^2=\frac12,\qquad d(H_0,X_D)=1,
     \qquad \angle_{H_0}(J,X_D)\ge\frac\pi2.$$ More precisely, all the fractional-point inequalities in Equation <a href="#energy:fractional-angle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:fractional-angle">[energy:fractional-angle]</a> hold.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-configuration"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-configuration</summary>

*Proof.* Use the normalized minimizing actions of Lemma <a href="#energy:minimizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="energy:minimizer">22</a> and the uniform bound of Lemma <a href="#energy:displacement-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="energy:displacement-bound">23</a>. The product construction, Lemma <a href="#energy:path-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="energy:path-bounds">26</a>, and the decoration-square argument give the first assertion. Lemmas <a href="#energy:hexagon-flatness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="energy:hexagon-flatness">28</a> and <a href="#energy:common-center" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="energy:common-center">29</a> give the second. The fixed-fraction argument above gives the third. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:obstruction"></a>
### The geometric obstruction

We now show that the configuration obtained in Proposition <a href="#prop:configuration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:configuration">30</a> cannot occur in a complete $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ space. The argument takes place first in that space and then in its completed tangent cone at the common hexagon center. The tangent cone is not assumed to be a vector space, so we specify the meaning of its sums and use concavity whenever an additive inequality is required.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Cone notation and square rigidity

<a id="geom:cone-addition"></a>
For a $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ Euclidean cone $T$ with vertex $0$, let $\delta_a$ denote radial dilation by $a\geq 0$. Write $a u=\delta_a(u)$ and $$\lVert u\rVert=d_T(0,u),\qquad
 \langle u,v\rangle=\frac{\lVert u\rVert^2+\lVert v\rVert^2-d_T(u,v)^2}{2}.$$ For nonzero vectors this last expression is $\lVert u\rVert\lVert v\rVert\cos\angle(u,v)$, where the angular distance is truncated at $\pi$. Define $$u\oplus v=2\operatorname{mid}(u,v).
 \label{geom:cone-addition}$$ This operation is commutative, but no associativity or cancellation law will be used. If the directions of $u,v$ are joined by a geodesic of length less than $\pi$, its cone is a Euclidean sector, and $u\oplus v$ is the ordinary vector sum in that sector. More generally, all two-vector calculations below are either made in such a sector or follow from the metric definition in Equation <a href="#geom:cone-addition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:cone-addition">[geom:cone-addition]</a>.

<a id="geom:sum-concavity"></a>
The scalar-product concavity established in Lemma <a href="#energy:cone-mean" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="energy:cone-mean">24</a> implies $$\langle w,u\oplus v\rangle\geq \langle w,u\rangle+\langle w,v\rangle.
 \label{geom:sum-concavity}$$ Indeed, apply concavity at the midpoint of $u,v$ and use homogeneity under radial dilation. This is an inequality in a general cone; we will not replace it by an equality without establishing rigidity.

We use two established equality principles for $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ spaces. A triangle is flat if one vertex angle equals its Euclidean comparison angle, or if a nontrivial interior comparison inequality is an equality; see (Bridson and Haefliger 1999, Proposition II.2.9 and Exercise II.2.10(1)) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[BH1999](#ref-BH1999)] and (Ballmann 1995, Proposition I.3.13(ii)). Here and below, flat means that the convex hull is isometric to the corresponding Euclidean triangle.

<a id="stmt-geom-square"></a>
<div id="geom:square" class="lemma">

**Lemma 31** (Square rigidity). *Let $p_0,p_1,p_2,p_3$ be points of a $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ space, with indices taken modulo four. Suppose $$d(p_i,p_{i+1})=1,\qquad
 d(p_0,p_2)=d(p_1,p_3)=\sqrt{2}.$$ Their convex hull is a unit Euclidean square, in the indicated cyclic order. In particular, the two diagonals have the same midpoint.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-square"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-square</summary>

*Proof.* Let $o$ be the midpoint of $p_0,p_2$. Squared-distance convexity gives $$d(o,p_1)^2\leq\frac12,\qquad d(o,p_3)^2\leq\frac12.$$ Since $$\sqrt2=d(p_1,p_3)\leq d(p_1,o)+d(o,p_3)\leq\sqrt2,$$ both inequalities are equalities and $o$ is also the midpoint of $p_1,p_3$. The equality for $d(o,p_1)$ is an interior comparison equality in the triangle $p_1,p_0,p_2$, so that triangle is flat. The same argument, with each of the four vertices as the vertex opposite a diagonal midpoint, makes each triangle on three consecutive vertices flat. The angle at each corner of the quadrilateral is therefore $\pi/2$. The flat quadrilateral theorem (Bridson and Haefliger 1999, Theorem II.2.11) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[BH1999](#ref-BH1999)] applies: the sum of these four angles is $2\pi$. Its Euclidean quadrilateral has the prescribed sides and diagonals, and hence is a unit square. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### The sums at the four doubled points

Suppose that $Z$ is a complete $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ space containing the configuration from Proposition <a href="#prop:configuration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:configuration">30</a>. Use the graph vertex names for the corresponding points of $Z$. Let $c$ be the common hexagon center and put $$r=\frac{\sqrt3}{2},\qquad T=T_c Z.$$ The completed tangent cone $T$ is $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$, and its completed space of directions is $\operatorname{CAT}(1)$ by (Bridson and Haefliger 1999, Theorem II.3.19) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[BH1999](#ref-BH1999)]. Write $$x_i=\log_c X_i,\qquad y_i=\log_c Y_i,\qquad
 h_H=\log_c H$$ for the named radial vectors, and abbreviate $h_{ij}=h_H$ when $H=ij$. All these vectors have norm $r$.

<a id="geom:line-products"></a>
Any two distinct line vertices $H,H'$ occur together in a core hexagon: take their intersection point $i$, choose a point $j\ne i$ on $H$ and a point $k\ne i$ on $H'$, and use the $X$ representatives of the noncollinear triple $i,j,k$. Their central triangle is flat by Proposition <a href="#prop:configuration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:configuration">30</a>, and their distance is $\sqrt2$. Consequently $$\langle h_H,h_{H'}\rangle=-\frac14
 \quad\text{for distinct lines }H,H'.
 \label{geom:line-products}$$

A line through a doubled point $i\in\{A,B,C,D\}$ is called *full at $i$* if it is adjacent to both $X_i$ and $Y_i$. All lines through $A,B,C$ are full; the full lines at $D$ are $AD,BD$.

<a id="stmt-geom-fan-sums"></a>
<div id="geom:fan-sums" class="lemma">

<a id="geom:fan-products"></a>
**Lemma 32** (Fan identities). *For $i\in\{A,B,C,D\}$ there is a unit vector $M_i\in T$ such that <a id="geom:fan-identities"></a>$$M_i=x_i\oplus y_i=h_H\oplus h_{H'}
 \quad\text{whenever }H,H'\text{ are distinct full lines at }i.
 \label{geom:fan-identities}$$ Each sum displayed here is computed in a nondegenerate Euclidean sector. Moreover, $$\langle x_i,y_i\rangle=-\frac14,
 \qquad
 \langle M_i,h_H\rangle=\langle M_i,h_{H'}\rangle=\frac12.
 \label{geom:fan-products}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-fan-sums"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-fan-sums</summary>

*Proof.* The four-cycle $X_i,H,Y_i,H'$ has unit sides and diagonals of length $\sqrt2$, by the two-step assertions of Proposition <a href="#prop:configuration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:configuration">30</a>. Lemma <a href="#geom:square" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:square">31</a> makes it a flat square. Let $J_i$ be its center, which is the midpoint of $X_i,Y_i$ and also of $H,H'$.

The triangle $c,H,H'$ is flat. Its median therefore gives $$d(c,J_i)^2=r^2-\frac14 d(H,H')^2=\frac14.$$ On the other hand, squared-distance convexity in the triangle $c,X_i,Y_i$ has right-hand side $$\frac12 d(c,X_i)^2+\frac12 d(c,Y_i)^2
       -\frac14 d(X_i,Y_i)^2
 =\frac14.$$ Thus this is also an equality, and $c,X_i,Y_i$ is a flat triangle. Taking its midpoint in the tangent cone proves $$x_i\oplus y_i=2\log_c J_i=h_H\oplus h_{H'}.$$ Define $M_i=2\log_c J_i$; it has norm one. The left-hand side makes it independent of the full-line pair chosen. The cosine law gives $\langle x_i,y_i\rangle=-1/4$. The pair $h_H,h_{H'}$ has angular separation $\arccos(-1/3)<\pi$, and in its Euclidean sector $$\langle h_H\oplus h_{H'},h_H\rangle
 =\lVert h_H\rVert^2+\langle h_{H'},h_H\rangle
 =\frac34-\frac14=\frac12.$$ The same computation applies to $h_{H'}$. ◻

</details>

<a id="geom:nonnegative"></a>
We will obtain a contradiction by evaluating the scalar product of the unit vectors $M_A,M_B$ in two ways. One bound is already available. At $A,B$, the fan identities give $$M_A=h_{AB}\oplus h_{AC},\qquad
 M_B=h_{AB}\oplus h_{BC}.$$ Apply Equation <a href="#geom:sum-concavity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:sum-concavity">[geom:sum-concavity]</a> in both entries. The three lines $AB,AC,BC$ are distinct, so Equation <a href="#geom:line-products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:line-products">[geom:line-products]</a> yields $$\langle M_A,M_B\rangle
 \geq\lVert h_{AB}\rVert^2+\langle h_{AB},h_{BC}\rangle
       +\langle h_{AC},h_{AB}\rangle+\langle h_{AC},h_{BC}\rangle
 =0.
 \label{geom:nonnegative}$$ The remaining argument will force $M_A,M_B$ to be antipodal, with scalar product $-1$. We first use the exceptional incidence to show that $M_C,M_D$ are antipodal. Their axis will let us place $h_{AC},h_{BC},h_{AD},h_{BD}$ in a product $\mathbb R\times Q$, where the $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ factor $Q$ contains a flat square formed by their second coordinates. The opposite side midpoints of that square will then force $M_A,M_B$ to be antipodal through the fan identities at $A,B$.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### The identity at the exceptional incidence

Set $$H_0=CD,\qquad h_0=h_{CD},\qquad
 p=X_C,\quad q=Y_C,\quad z=X_D,$$ and let $J$ be the midpoint of $p,q$. The missing edge from $Y_D$ to $H_0$ is responsible for the asymmetry used in the next lemma.

<a id="stmt-geom-exceptional"></a>
<div id="geom:exceptional" class="lemma">

<a id="geom:exceptional-products"></a>
**Lemma 33** (Exceptional-incidence identity). *The vectors $M_C,x_D$ lie in a Euclidean sector in $T$, and <a id="geom:exceptional-sum"></a>$$h_0=M_C\oplus x_D.
 \label{geom:exceptional-sum}$$ Within this sector, $$\langle M_C,x_D\rangle=-\frac12,\qquad
 \langle M_C,h_0\rangle=\frac12,\qquad
 \langle h_0,x_D\rangle=\frac14.
 \label{geom:exceptional-products}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-exceptional"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-exceptional</summary>

<a id="geom:exceptional-distances"></a>
*Proof.* Apply the square construction in Lemma <a href="#geom:fan-sums" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:fan-sums">32</a> at $C$, using $H_0$ and any other line through $C$. Since $J$ is its center, $$d(H_0,J)^2=\frac12,\qquad d(c,J)^2=\frac14.
 \label{geom:exceptional-distances}$$ Also $d(H_0,z)=1$, $d(c,z)=d(c,H_0)=r$, and the two paths $p,H_0,z$ and $q,H_0,z$ give $$d(p,z)^2=d(q,z)^2=2.$$ The ternary midpoint conclusion of Proposition <a href="#prop:configuration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:configuration">30</a> states that the angle at $H_0$ between the segments to $J,z$ is at least $\pi/2$. Angle comparison and Equation <a href="#geom:exceptional-distances" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:exceptional-distances">[geom:exceptional-distances]</a> therefore imply $$d(J,z)^2\geq d(H_0,J)^2+d(H_0,z)^2=\frac32.$$ The reverse inequality follows from the fact that $J$ is the midpoint of $p,q$: $$d(J,z)^2\leq\frac12d(p,z)^2+\frac12d(q,z)^2
                    -\frac14d(p,q)^2=\frac32.$$ The angle and distance comparisons are consequently equalities. The triangle $H_0,J,z$ is flat, with a right angle at $H_0$.

Let $B_0$ be the point of $[J,z]$ at parameter $1/3$ from $J$. Calculating in this right triangle gives $$d(H_0,B_0)^2
 =\left(\frac23\right)^2\frac12
  +\left(\frac13\right)^2=\frac13.$$ Squared-distance convexity toward $c$ gives $$\begin{aligned}
 d(c,B_0)^2
 &\leq\frac23 d(c,J)^2+\frac13d(c,z)^2
                 -\frac29d(J,z)^2\\
 &=\frac23\frac14+\frac13\frac34-\frac29\frac32
 =\frac1{12}.
\end{aligned}$$ The triangle inequality now reads $$\frac{\sqrt3}{2}=d(H_0,c)
 \leq d(H_0,B_0)+d(B_0,c)
 \leq\frac1{\sqrt3}+\frac1{2\sqrt3}
 =\frac{\sqrt3}{2}.$$ Hence every inequality is an equality. In particular, $B_0$ lies on $[H_0,c]$, in that order, and the triangle $c,J,z$ is flat by its interior comparison equality. Put $j=\log_c J$. In the Euclidean sector containing $j,x_D$, the vector to $B_0$ is $(2/3)j+(1/3)x_D$. The collinearity just proved and the distances $d(c,H_0)=3d(c,B_0)$ therefore imply $$h_0=3\log_c B_0=2j+x_D=M_C\oplus x_D.$$ Here the two ordinary additions take place in that Euclidean sector, and $M_C=2j$ by Lemma <a href="#geom:fan-sums" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:fan-sums">32</a>.

The norm identity in this sector gives $$\frac34=\lVert h_0\rVert^2
 =1+\frac34+2\langle M_C,x_D\rangle,$$ so $\langle M_C,x_D\rangle=-1/2$. Pairing the sector identity with either summand yields the remaining two products in Equation <a href="#geom:exceptional-products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:exceptional-products">[geom:exceptional-products]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### A semicircle in the space of directions

<a id="stmt-geom-antipodal"></a>
<div id="geom:antipodal" class="lemma">

**Lemma 34**. *The unit vectors $M_C,M_D$ are antipodal in $T$: $$d_T(M_C,M_D)=2,\qquad \langle M_C,M_D\rangle=-1.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-antipodal"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-antipodal</summary>

*Proof.* Take either full line $H$ at $D$. The path $Y_D,H,X_D,H_0$ uses two fields of $F_D$ and the loose field, so it is one of the admissible three-step paths. Thus $$d(Y_D,H_0)\geq\sqrt3=d(Y_D,c)+d(c,H_0).$$ Equality follows from the triangle inequality. The segment from $Y_D$ to $H_0$ passes through $c$, and the directions of $y_D,h_0$ are antipodal.

Let $\Sigma$ be the completed space of directions of $Z$ at $c$, and use a hat for the direction of a nonzero vector. Define $$a=\arccos(1/\sqrt3),\qquad b=\arccos(1/3).$$ Both lie strictly between zero and $\pi/2$, and $2a+b=\pi$. Equation <a href="#geom:exceptional-products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:exceptional-products">[geom:exceptional-products]</a> gives $$d_\Sigma(\widehat M_C,\widehat h_0)=a,
 \qquad d_\Sigma(\widehat h_0,\widehat x_D)=b.$$ The two arcs occur consecutively in the Euclidean sector of Lemma <a href="#geom:exceptional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:exceptional">33</a>; their concatenation is its geodesic direction arc. By Equation <a href="#geom:fan-products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:fan-products">[geom:fan-products]</a>, $$d_\Sigma(\widehat x_D,\widehat y_D)=\arccos(-1/3)=\pi-b.$$ The direction of $M_D=x_D\oplus y_D$ bisects this last arc, because its two summands have equal length. Consequently $d_\Sigma(\widehat x_D,\widehat M_D)=a$.

The concatenation $\widehat h_0,\widehat x_D,\widehat y_D$ has length $\pi$ and antipodal endpoints. It is therefore minimizing, so its two arcs continue geodesically through $\widehat x_D$. It follows that $$\widehat M_C,\quad\widehat h_0,\quad\widehat x_D,
 \quad\widehat M_D$$ is a local geodesic in $\Sigma$: continuation at $\widehat h_0$ holds inside the first sector, and continuation at $\widehat x_D$ holds inside the minimizing semicircle just described. Its length is $a+b+a=\pi$. The space $\Sigma$ is $\operatorname{CAT}(1)$, so (Bridson and Haefliger 1999, Proposition II.1.4(2)) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[BH1999](#ref-BH1999)] makes this local geodesic globally minimizing, including the endpoint case of length exactly $\pi$. Equivalently, apply the short local-geodesic assertion to every strict initial subarc and pass to the endpoint by continuity of distance. Its endpoints are antipodal. Since $M_C,M_D$ have norm one, the cone distance and scalar product have the stated values. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### Splitting the axis and obtaining a flat square

<a id="stmt-prop-no-configuration"></a>
<div id="prop:no-configuration" class="proposition">

**Proposition 35**. *No complete $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ space contains the configuration asserted in Proposition <a href="#prop:configuration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:configuration">30</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-no-configuration"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-no-configuration</summary>

<a id="geom:axis-products"></a>
*Proof.* Suppose otherwise and retain the notation above. By Equation <a href="#geom:nonnegative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:nonnegative">[geom:nonnegative]</a>, $\langle M_A,M_B\rangle\geq0$. We will contradict this bound by splitting the axis supplied by Lemma <a href="#geom:antipodal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:antipodal">34</a>. Use the fan identities $$M_C=h_{AC}\oplus h_{BC},\qquad
 M_D=h_{AD}\oplus h_{BD}.$$ The four lines in these expressions are pairwise distinct. Therefore Equation <a href="#geom:sum-concavity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:sum-concavity">[geom:sum-concavity]</a> implies $$\langle M_C,h_{AD}\rangle\geq-\frac12,
 \qquad \langle M_C,h_{BD}\rangle\geq-\frac12.$$ Lemma <a href="#geom:antipodal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:antipodal">34</a> forces equality in the chain $$-1=\langle M_C,M_D\rangle
 \geq\langle M_C,h_{AD}\rangle+\langle M_C,h_{BD}\rangle\geq-1.$$ Interchanging the roles of the two sums gives the symmetric equalities. Combining these with Equation <a href="#geom:fan-products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:fan-products">[geom:fan-products]</a>, we obtain $$\begin{array}{c|rrrr}
 &h_{AC}&h_{BC}&h_{AD}&h_{BD}\\ \hline
 \langle M_C,\,\cdot\,\rangle& 1/2&1/2&-1/2&-1/2\\
 \langle M_D,\,\cdot\,\rangle&-1/2&-1/2& 1/2& 1/2.
\end{array}
\label{geom:axis-products}$$

The opposite rays $M_C,M_D$ form a complete geodesic line $\ell\subset T$ through the cone vertex. Put $a=\arccos(1/\sqrt3)$. For each vector in the four columns of Equation <a href="#geom:axis-products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:axis-products">[geom:axis-products]</a>, the angles to the two ends of $\ell$ are $a$ and $\pi-a$, in one order or the other. The two corresponding direction arcs concatenate to a minimizing semicircle. Its Euclidean cone is an isometric flat half-plane with boundary $\ell$ and containing the vector. In particular, that vector lies on a complete line parallel to $\ell$.

<a id="geom:split-coordinates"></a>
The parallel-set product decomposition (Bridson and Haefliger 1999, Theorem II.2.14) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[BH1999](#ref-BH1999)] now applies in $T$; it requires no properness. Write its convex parallel set as $$P(\ell)=\mathbb R\times Q,\qquad
 \ell=\mathbb R\times\{q_0\},\qquad
 0=(0,q_0),\quad M_C=(1,q_0),\quad M_D=(-1,q_0).$$ The factor $Q$ is a $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ space. Within each of the flat half-planes above, projection onto $\ell$ shows that the axial coordinate is the scalar product with the unit vector $M_C$. Thus Equation <a href="#geom:axis-products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:axis-products">[geom:axis-products]</a> gives $$h_{AC}=(1/2,u),\quad h_{BC}=(1/2,u'),\quad
 h_{AD}=(-1/2,v),\quad h_{BD}=(-1/2,v')
 \label{geom:split-coordinates}$$ for points $u,u',v,v'\in Q$. Their distances to $q_0$ are all $1/\sqrt2$, since each original vector has squared norm $3/4$.

Distinct line vectors have squared cone distance two, by Equation <a href="#geom:line-products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:line-products">[geom:line-products]</a>. The product metric in Equation <a href="#geom:split-coordinates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:split-coordinates">[geom:split-coordinates]</a> therefore gives $$d_Q(u,u')=d_Q(v,v')=\sqrt2,$$ and $$d_Q(u,v)=d_Q(u,v')=d_Q(u',v)=d_Q(u',v')=1.$$ Furthermore, $h_{AC}\oplus h_{BC}=M_C$ means that the midpoint of these two line vectors is $(1/2,q_0)$: the midpoint is the half-dilation of $M_C$ on the axis. Product midpoints consequently give $q_0=\operatorname{mid}(u,u')$. The identity at $D$ likewise gives $q_0=\operatorname{mid}(v,v')$.

<a id="geom:opposite-midpoints"></a>
In cyclic order $u,v,u',v'$, these points satisfy Lemma <a href="#geom:square" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:square">31</a>. They therefore span a unit flat square in $Q$, centered at $q_0$. Let $$m=\operatorname{mid}(u,v),\qquad
 m'=\operatorname{mid}(u',v').$$ They are the midpoints of opposite sides of that square, and hence $$d_Q(q_0,m)=d_Q(q_0,m')=\frac12,
 \qquad d_Q(m,m')=1.
 \label{geom:opposite-midpoints}$$ This square lies in the transverse factor only; no planar realization of the original incidence configuration has been assumed. Figure <a href="#fig:transverse-square" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:transverse-square">3</a> records the square and the axis that has been split off.

<figure id="fig:transverse-square">
<p>$P(ℓ) ≅ ℝ × Q$</p>
<p>$M_{A} = 2(0,m),   M_{B} = 2(0,m′)$</p>
<figcaption>The final contradiction takes place in the transverse factor after splitting off the $M_{C}, M_{D}$ axis. The cyclically ordered points $u, v, u′, v′$ form a flat unit square in $Q$, with common diagonal midpoint $q_{0}$. The opposite-side midpoints $m, m′$ are distance $1/2$ from $q_{0}$ and distance $1$ apart. Dilation in the original tangent cone $T$ therefore makes $M_{A} = 2(0,m)$ and $M_{B} = 2(0,m′)$ antipodal unit vectors. The panels display the two distinct factors of the parallel set.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-transverse-square"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:transverse-square`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=3.3cm,y=3.3cm,

  square label/.style={font=\small,fill=white,inner sep=1.5pt}]
  \coordinate (u) at (-.5,.5);
  \coordinate (v) at (.5,.5);
  \coordinate (up) at (.5,-.5);
  \coordinate (vp) at (-.5,-.5);

  \fill[blue!3] (u)--(v)--(up)--(vp)--cycle;

  \draw[line width=.85pt] (u)--(v)--(up)--(vp)--cycle;
  \draw[draw=blue!65!black,line width=1.2pt] (m)--(mp);

    \node[square vertex] at (\P) {};

  \node[square label,anchor=west] at (.025,-.25) {$\frac12$};

\begin{tikzpicture}[x=2.05cm,y=1cm,
  axis label/.style={font=\small,fill=white,inner sep=1.5pt}]
  \draw[{Stealth[length=4pt]}-{Stealth[length=4pt]},line width=.7pt]
    (-1.22,0)--(1.22,0);
  \foreach \x in {-1,0,1}\fill (\x,0) circle[radius=1.65pt];
  \node[axis label,anchor=south] at (-1,.07) {$M_D$};
  \node[axis label,anchor=south] at (1,.07) {$M_C$};
  \node[axis label,anchor=north] at (0,-.07) {$0_T$};
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 39 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Two panels illustrating factors of the parallel set. Left panel titled "Transverse factor $Q$": a unit square with vertices $u$ (top left), $v$ (top right), $u'$ (bottom right), and $v'$ (bottom left). Dashed diagonals intersect at center point $q_0$. Midpoints of top and bottom edges are labeled $m$ and $m'$. A dashed vertical line segment connects $m$ to $q_0$ and $q_0$ to $m'$, each segment labeled $\frac{1}{2}$. The right edge has a dimension label $1$. Right panel: a horizontal line labeled above as "Axis $\ell = \mathbb{R} \times \{q_0\}$", containing marked points $M_D$ (left), $0_T$ (center), and $M_C$ (right). Below the axis are the formulas "$P(\ell) \cong \mathbb{R} \times Q$" and "$M_A = 2(0, m), \quad M_B = 2(0, m')$".
```


Finally, the full-line choices $AC,AD$ at $A$ and $BC,BD$ at $B$ give $$M_A=h_{AC}\oplus h_{AD}=\delta_2(0,m),\qquad
 M_B=h_{BC}\oplus h_{BD}=\delta_2(0,m').$$ Here $\delta_2$ is dilation in the original cone $T$; no cone structure on $Q$ is needed. It doubles all distances. By Equation <a href="#geom:opposite-midpoints" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:opposite-midpoints">[geom:opposite-midpoints]</a>, $$\lVert M_A\rVert=\lVert M_B\rVert=1,\qquad d_T(M_A,M_B)=2,$$ so $\langle M_A,M_B\rangle=-1$. This contradicts Equation <a href="#geom:nonnegative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:nonnegative">[geom:nonnegative]</a> and proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:completion"></a>
### Selection and the finite-model conclusion

We now assemble the arguments, keeping the order of all choices explicit.

<a id="stmt-lem-completion-diagonal"></a>
<div id="lem:completion-diagonal" class="lemma">

**Lemma 36** (A deterministic diagonal sequence). *There is a sequence of realizations of the tables, with $n_j\to\infty$ and positive parameters $\alpha_j,\beta_j,\delta_j\to0$, such that*

1.  *the associated complexes $P_j$ are aspherical and have a linear letter disk-filling inequality;*

2.  *the master match sets are nonempty and their single-field marginals tend to uniformity in total variation;*

3.  *for every admissible pair $I,\{e\}$ and every fixed integer $t$, the common-$I$ coupling in Proposition <a href="#prop:sampling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:sampling">20</a> has both of its total variation errors tending to zero along the sequence;*

4.  *the six-letter table pair counts have uniformly vanishing relative error.*

*The groups $G_j=\pi_1P_j$ are infinite.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-completion-diagonal"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-completion-diagonal</summary>

*Proof.* At stage $j$, request all the admissible tests in the fixed master pattern with $1\le t\le j$. This is a finite list. Choose $\varepsilon_j>0$ small enough for Proposition <a href="#prop:sampling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:sampling">20</a> for this list and for Theorem <a href="#thm:diagram" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:diagram">7</a>, with $\varepsilon_j<1/(400j)$, and set $$\alpha_j=\delta_j=\varepsilon_j,
 \qquad \beta_j=8\varepsilon_j.$$ These parameters satisfy $\beta_j>7\alpha_j$, and the exponent $3-\beta_j-90\delta_j$ of the master count is positive. Moreover, $$j(\beta_j+90\delta_j)=98j\varepsilon_j<\frac{98}{400}<\frac12,$$ so the explicit bound in Section <a href="#sec:sampling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:sampling">4</a> preserves every overlap margin required for the finite list.

Fix these parameters. The proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:diagram" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:diagram">7</a> selects a finite fragment threshold, possibly depending on $j$. Its coincidence inequalities hold with probability tending to one as $n\to\infty$. For the same fixed parameters, Proposition <a href="#prop:sampling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:sampling">20</a> gives the requested finite collection of sampling properties with probability tending to one. Choose $n_j>n_{j-1}$ so large that there is positive probability that all these events hold and all requested sampling errors are at most $1/j$. Select one realization in their intersection. No comparison between the fragment threshold and $n$ is needed before fixing the parameters and threshold. This proves <span class="upright">(i)</span>–<span class="upright">(iv)</span>.

Each $G_j$ has a quotient $C_2$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:model-presentation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:model-presentation">4</a>. If it were finite, its universal cover would be a finite contractible CW complex, and multiplicativity of Euler characteristic would give $$1=\chi(\widetilde P_j)=|G_j|\chi(P_j).$$ Since $\chi(P_j)$ is an integer, this forces $|G_j|=1$, contrary to the quotient. Hence $G_j$ is infinite. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-completion-no-action"></a>
<div id="prop:completion-no-action" class="proposition">

**Proposition 37**. *For all sufficiently large $j$, the group $G_j$ in Lemma <a href="#lem:completion-diagonal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:completion-diagonal">36</a> admits no geometric action on any proper complete $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ space.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-completion-no-action"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-completion-no-action</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose, more generally, that infinitely many $G_j$ admitted such actions. Restrict to that subsequence and choose one action for each of its groups. All fixed-test limits in Lemma <a href="#lem:completion-diagonal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:completion-diagonal">36</a> remain valid. The action spaces, their dimensions, and their local geometries are allowed to vary.

Normalize the minimizing generator energy for each action as in Equation <a href="#energy:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="energy:normalization">[energy:normalization]</a>. The infinitude just proved makes this energy positive. The displacement estimate and Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-test">25</a> hold uniformly for these choices of actions. Proposition <a href="#prop:configuration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:configuration">30</a> therefore supplies the limiting configuration. Proposition <a href="#prop:no-configuration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:no-configuration">35</a> excludes it. Thus only finitely many of the selected groups can admit such actions; in particular, an instance with no action exists. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### Passing to a finite simplicial model

We record the filling transfer because the conclusion is about edge loops in a finite simplicial complex, rather than just words in a presentation.

<a id="stmt-lem-completion-transfer"></a>
<div id="lem:completion-transfer" class="lemma">

**Lemma 38** (Finite-model filling transfer). *Let $P$ be a finite connected CW complex with contractible universal cover. Suppose a finite presentation of $\pi_1P$ has a linear disk-filling inequality. Then $P$ has a finite connected simplicial homotopy model $K$, whose universal cover is contractible and satisfies a linear combinatorial disk-filling inequality.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-completion-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-completion-transfer</summary>

*Proof.* A finite CW complex has a finite simplicial homotopy model of the same dimension (Hatcher 2002, Theorem 2C.5) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[Hatcher2002](#ref-Hatcher2002)]. One construction proceeds over its finitely many cells: approximate attaching maps by simplicial maps after finite subdivisions, and use simplicial analogues of their mapping cylinders for the attachments. Let $K$ be the resulting finite simplicial model. A homotopy equivalence lifts to a homotopy equivalence of universal covers, so $\widetilde K$ is contractible.

For completeness, the linear estimate is independent of the chosen finite presentation. Choose substitutions $\phi$ from new generators to old words and $\psi$ from old generators to new words, inducing inverse group identifications. Let $L$ bound the lengths of the words $\phi(b)$, let $M$ bound the new-presentation filling areas of the finitely many substituted old relators $\psi(r)$, and let $D$ bound the filling areas of the words $b\psi(\phi(b))^{-1}$. The latter two lists consist of null words, and all three lists are finite, so these bounds exist. A new null word of length $\ell$ substitutes to an old word of length at most $L\ell$. If the old filling constant is $C_{\rm old}$, filling that word and replacing its relator cells costs at most $MC_{\rm old}L\ell$ new cells. The boundary substitutions cost at most $D\ell$ more. Thus the new filling constant is at most $MC_{\rm old}L+D$.

Apply this to the maximal-tree presentation of the finite complex $K$. Its generators correspond to the non-tree edges, and its relators to its triangular faces. A null-homotopic combinatorial edge loop in $K$ of length $\ell$ gives a null word of length at most $\ell$ in these generators after inserting paths in the fixed maximal tree. The tree has finitely many edges, so the inserted paths have uniformly bounded lengths. Fill the word linearly in the maximal-tree presentation; each relator cell is replaced by the corresponding triangular face and tree-path cancellations. The boundary collar likewise cancels the inserted tree paths with linear overhead. Thus the original loop bounds a diagram over $K$ with at most $C\ell$ triangular faces for a fixed finite $C$.

An edge loop in $\widetilde K$ projects to a null loop in $K$. Its disk diagram lifts with the chosen boundary lift, giving the required estimate in the universal cover. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-completion-transfer-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-completion-transfer</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Choose an instance $P_j$ supplied by Proposition <a href="#prop:completion-no-action" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:completion-no-action">37</a>. Its finite letter presentation has a linear disk bound by Theorem <a href="#thm:diagram" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:diagram">7</a>. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:completion-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:completion-transfer">38</a> to obtain $K$. Since $P_j$ has dimension two, the same-dimension construction gives a two-dimensional $K$. This proves the asserted asphericity and linear filling estimate. Its fundamental group is $G_j$, so it has the no-action property. The equivalence between a linear Dehn function and word hyperbolicity gives the final group-theoretic assertion (Bridson and Haefliger 1999, Exercise III.H.2.5(5), Proposition III.H.2.7, and Theorem III.H.2.9) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[BH1999](#ref-BH1999)].

Suppose that a finite simplicial complex $L$ homotopy equivalent to $K$ admitted a compatible locally $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ geodesic length metric. The metric space $L$ is compact, hence complete and locally compact. Give its universal cover the induced length metric, for which the covering map is a local isometry (Bridson and Haefliger 1999, Proposition I.3.25) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[BH1999](#ref-BH1999)]. This cover is complete: a Cauchy sequence upstairs projects to a convergent sequence downstairs; sufficiently short connecting paths force a tail into one sheet over an evenly covered neighborhood of the limit, where it converges. The Cartan–Hadamard theorem makes the cover globally $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ (Bridson and Haefliger 1999, Theorem II.4.1(2)) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[BH1999](#ref-BH1999)]. It is locally compact, and the length-space Hopf–Rinow theorem makes it proper (Bridson and Haefliger 1999, Proposition I.3.7) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[BH1999](#ref-BH1999)]. Its deck transformations act freely, properly and cocompactly by isometries.

The isomorphism $\pi_1L\cong\pi_1K=G_j$ therefore gives a geometric action of $G_j$ on a proper complete $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ space, a contradiction. This also excludes compatible locally $\operatorname{CAT}(-1)$ geodesic length metrics, since a $\operatorname{CAT}(-1)$ space is $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ (Bridson and Haefliger 1999, Theorem II.1.12(1)) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[BH1999](#ref-BH1999)]. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-1"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 39*. The argument makes no curvature assumption on the random complexes $P_j$. Curvature comparison is used only in a hypothetical action space, after the combinatorial filling and asphericity statements have been established. Nor is a common filling constant needed for the diagonal sequence: only the constant for the single chosen finite complex appears in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

</div>

```json
  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The seven points and seven lines of the Fano plane. The three sides, three medians, and dashed circle each represent one projective line; only marked dots represent projective points. The points $A, B, C, D = A + B + C$ form a quadrangle: no three are collinear. The thick median is the line $CD = {C, D, A + B}$, at which the incidence graph is modified.",
      "figure_id": "fig:fano-template",
      "pdf_page": 6,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:fano-template",
          "exact_quote": "The seven points and seven lines of the Fano plane.  The three sides,\nthree medians, and dashed circle each represent one projective line; only\nmarked dots represent projective points.  The\npoints $A,B,C,D=A+B+C$ form a quadrangle: no three",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.25cm,y=1.25cm,\n  fano edge/.style={draw=black!75,line width=.65pt},\n  fano point/.style={circle,fill=black,inner sep=1.65pt},\n  fano label/.style={font=\\small,fill=white,inner sep=1.5pt}]\n  \\coordinate (A) at ({-sqrt(3)},-1);\n  \\coordinate (B) at ({sqrt(3)},-1);\n  \\coordinate (C) at (0,2);\n  \\coordinate (D) at (0,0);\n  \\coordinate (AB) at (0,-1);\n  \\coordinate (AC) at ({-sqrt(3)/2},.5);\n  \\coordinate (BC) at ({sqrt(3)/2},.5);\n  \\draw[fano edge] (A)--(B)--(C)--cycle;\n  \\draw[fano edge] (A)--(BC) (B)--(AC);\n  \\draw[fano edge,densely dashed] (D) circle[radius=1];\n  \\draw[draw=orange!75!black,line width=1.35pt] (C)--(AB);\n  \\foreach \\P in {A,B,C,D,AB,AC,BC}\n    \\node[fano point] at (\\P) {};\n  \\node[fano label,anchor=north east] at (A) {$A=e_1$};\n  \\node[fano label,anchor=north west] at (B) {$B=e_2$};\n  \\node[fano label,anchor=south] at (C) {$C=e_3$};\n  \\node[fano label,anchor=north west] at (.07,-.03) {$D$};\n  \\node[fano label,anchor=north] at (0,-1.08) {$A+B$};\n  \\node[fano label,anchor=east] at ({-sqrt(3)/2-.07},.5) {$A+C$};\n  \\node[fano label,anchor=west] at ({sqrt(3)/2+.07},.5) {$B+C$};\n  \\node[fano label,anchor=west,text=orange!75!black] at (.05,.65) {$CD$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A diagram of the Fano plane showing a triangle with vertices $C = e_3$ (top), $A = e_1$ (bottom left), and $B = e_2$ (bottom right). The midpoints of the triangle edges are marked with dots labeled $A + C$ (left edge), $B + C$ (right edge), and $A + B$ (bottom edge). A central dot is labeled $D$. The outer three edges of the triangle connect the vertices. Three median lines pass through $D$: from $A$ to $B + C$, from $B$ to $A + C$, and from $C$ through $D$ to $A + B$. The median line connecting $C$, $D$, and $A + B$ is drawn as a thick orange line and labeled $CD$. A dashed circle passes through the three edge midpoints $A + B$, $B + C$, and $A + C$."
    },

    {
      "caption": "$P(ℓ) ≅ ℝ × Q$ $M_{A} = 2(0,m), M_{B} = 2(0,m′)$ The final contradiction takes place in the transverse factor after splitting off the $M_{C}, M_{D}$ axis. The cyclically ordered points $u, v, u′, v′$ form a flat unit square in $Q$, with common diagonal midpoint $q_{0}$. The opposite-side midpoints $m, m′$ are distance $1/2$ from $q_{0}$ and distance $1$ apart. Dilation in the original tangent cone $T$ therefore makes $M_{A} = 2(0,m)$ and $M_{B} = 2(0,m′)$ antipodal unit vectors. The panels display the two distinct factors of the parallel set.",

          "source_object": "fig:0003"

      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=3.3cm,y=3.3cm,\n  square vertex/.style={circle,fill=black,inner sep=1.65pt},\n  square label/.style={font=\\small,fill=white,inner sep=1.5pt}]\n  \\coordinate (u) at (-.5,.5);\n  \\coordinate (v) at (.5,.5);\n  \\coordinate (up) at (.5,-.5);\n  \\coordinate (vp) at (-.5,-.5);\n  \\coordinate (m) at (0,.5);\n  \\coordinate (mp) at (0,-.5);\n  \\coordinate (q) at (0,0);\n  \\fill[blue!3] (u)--(v)--(up)--(vp)--cycle;\n  \\draw[draw=black!40,densely dashed] (u)--(up) (v)--(vp);\n  \\draw[line width=.85pt] (u)--(v)--(up)--(vp)--cycle;\n  \\draw[draw=blue!65!black,line width=1.2pt] (m)--(mp);\n  \\foreach \\P in {u,v,up,vp,m,mp,q}\n    \\node[square vertex] at (\\P) {};\n  \\node[square label,anchor=south east] at (u) {$u$};\n  \\node[square label,anchor=south west] at (v) {$v$};\n  \\node[square label,anchor=north west] at (up) {$u'$};\n  \\node[square label,anchor=north east] at (vp) {$v'$};\n  \\node[square label,anchor=south] at (0,.52) {$m$};\n  \\node[square label,anchor=north] at (0,-.52) {$m'$};\n  \\node[square label,anchor=east] at (-.025,0) {$q_0$};\n  \\node[square label,anchor=west] at (.025,.25) {$\\frac12$};\n  \\node[square label,anchor=west] at (.025,-.25) {$\\frac12$};\n  \\node[square label,anchor=west] at (.54,0) {$1$};\n  \\node[font=\\small] at (0,.85) {Transverse factor $Q$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}\n\n\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=2.05cm,y=1cm,\n  axis label/.style={font=\\small,fill=white,inner sep=1.5pt}]\n  \\draw[{Stealth[length=4pt]}-{Stealth[length=4pt]},line width=.7pt]\n    (-1.22,0)--(1.22,0);\n  \\foreach \\x in {-1,0,1}\\fill (\\x,0) circle[radius=1.65pt];\n  \\node[axis label,anchor=south] at (-1,.07) {$M_D$};\n  \\node[axis label,anchor=south] at (1,.07) {$M_C$};\n  \\node[axis label,anchor=north] at (0,-.07) {$0_T$};\n  \\node[font=\\small] at (0,.9) {Axis $\\ell=\\mathbb R\\times\\{q_0\\}$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two panels illustrating factors of the parallel set. Left panel titled \"Transverse factor $Q$\": a unit square with vertices $u$ (top left), $v$ (top right), $u'$ (bottom right), and $v'$ (bottom left). Dashed diagonals intersect at center point $q_0$. Midpoints of top and bottom edges are labeled $m$ and $m'$. A dashed vertical line segment connects $m$ to $q_0$ and $q_0$ to $m'$, each segment labeled $\\frac{1}{2}$. The right edge has a dimension label $1$. Right panel: a horizontal line labeled above as \"Axis $\\ell = \\mathbb{R} \\times \\{q_0\\}$\", containing marked points $M_D$ (left), $0_T$ (center), and $M_C$ (right). Below the axis are the formulas \"$P(\\ell) \\cong \\mathbb{R} \\times Q$\" and \"$M_A = 2(0, m), \\quad M_B = 2(0, m')$\"."
    }
```
